The measures with L 2 L^{2} -bounded Riesz transform and the Painlevé problem Thanks: Both authors were supported by 2017-SGR-0395 (Catalonia) and MTM-2016-77635-P (MINECO, Spain). D.D. was supported by the Academy of Finland via the project Incidences on Fractals , grant No. 321896. X.T. was supported by the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement 101018680).
Damian Dąbrowski, Xavier Tolsa

Abstract
. In this work we provide a geometric characterization of the measures μ \mu in ℝ n + 1 {\mathbb{R}}^{n+1} with polynomial upper growth of degree n n such that the n n -dimensional Riesz transform ℛ ​ μ ​ ( x ) = ∫ x − y | x − y | n + 1 ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( y ) {\mathcal{R}}\mu(x)=\int\frac{x-y}{|x-y|^{n+1}}\,d\mu(y) belongs to L 2 ​ ( μ ) L^{2}(\mu) . More precisely, it is shown that ‖ ℛ ​ μ ‖ L 2 ​ ( μ ) 2 + ‖ μ ‖ ≈ ∫ ∫ 0 ∞ β 2 , μ ​ ( x , r ) 2 ​ μ ⁡ ( B ⁡ ( x , r ) ) r n ​ d ​ r r ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( x ) + ‖ μ ‖ , \|{\mathcal{R}}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\|\mu\|\approx\int\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta_{2,\mu}(x,r)^{2}\,\frac{\mu(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\mu(x)+\|\mu\|, where β μ , 2 ​ ( x , r ) 2 = inf L 1 r n ​ ∫ B ⁡ ( x , r ) ( dist ⁡ ( y , L ) r ) 2 ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( y ) , \beta_{\mu,2}(x,r)^{2}=\inf_{L}\frac{1}{r^{n}}\int_{B(x,r)}\left(\frac{{\rm dist}(y,L)}{r}\right)^{2}\,d\mu(y), with the infimum taken over all affine n n -planes L ⊂ ℝ n + 1 L\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1} . As a corollary, we obtain a characterization of the removable sets for Lipschitz harmonic functions in terms of a metric-geometric potential and we deduce that the class of removable sets for Lipschitz harmonic functions is invariant by bilipschitz mappings.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
42B20, 28A75, 49Q15

1.
Introduction

Given a Radon measure
\mu
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, its
(
n
-dimensional) Riesz transform at
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
is defined by

{\mathcal{R}}\mu(x)=\int\frac{x-y}{|x-y|^{n+1}}\,d\mu(y),

whenever the integral makes sense. For
f\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mu)
,
one writes
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}f(x)={\mathcal{R}}(f\mu)(x)
.
Given
{\varepsilon}>0
, the
{\varepsilon}
-truncated Riesz transform of
\mu
equals

{\mathcal{R}}_{\varepsilon}\mu(x)=\int_{|x-y|>{\varepsilon}}\frac{x-y}{|x-y|^{n+1}}\,d\mu(y),

and the operator
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu,{\varepsilon}}
is defined by

{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu,{\varepsilon}}f(x)={\mathcal{R}}_{\varepsilon}(f\mu)(x)
.

We say that
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
if
the operators
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu,{\varepsilon}}
are bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
uniformly on
{\varepsilon}
, and then we denote

\|{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}=\sup_{{\varepsilon}>0}\|{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu,{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}.

We also write

{\mathcal{R}}_{*}\mu(x)=\sup_{{\varepsilon}>0}|{\mathcal{R}}_{{\varepsilon}}\mu(x)|,\qquad\operatorname{pv}{\mathcal{R}}\mu(x)=\lim_{{\varepsilon}>0}{\mathcal{R}}_{{\varepsilon}}\mu(x),

in case that the latter limit exists. Sometimes we will abuse notation by writing
{\mathcal{R}}\mu
instead of
\operatorname{pv}{\mathcal{R}}\mu
.

This paper provides a full geometric description of the measures
\mu
with no point masses such that
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}
is bounded in

L^{2}(\mu)
.
In the case
n=1
, such description has already been obtained (see
[
MV
]
,
[
Lé
]
,
[
To1
]
), relying on the connection between Menger curvature and the Cauchy kernel
found by Melnikov
[
Me
]
. In higher dimensions, a similar connection is missing,
and thus obtaining analogous results presents major difficulties. In the case when the measure
\mu
is AD-regular (i.e., Ahlfors-David regular) that geometric description is equivalent to the
codimension
1
David-Semmes problem, solved by Nazarov, Tolsa, and Volberg
in
[
NToV1
]
. Recall that a measure
\mu
is AD-regular (or
n
-AD-regular) if there exists a constant
C>0
such that

C^{-1}\,r^{n}\leq\mu(B(x,r))\leq C\,r^{n}\quad\mbox{ for all $x\in\operatorname{supp}\mu$ and $0<r\leq{\rm diam}(\operatorname{supp}\mu)$.}

One of the main motivations for the description of the measures
\mu
such that
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)

is the characterization of the removable singularities for Lipschitz harmonic functions. Also, one may expect other
applications regarding the study of harmonic and elliptic measures. Indeed, in some of the recent advances on this topic, the connection between harmonic measure, the Riesz transform, and rectifiability has played an essential role (see
[
AHM+
]
,
[
AMT
]
, and
[
AMTV
]
, for example).

Next we need to introduce additional notation. For a ball
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, we consider its
n
-dimensional density (with respect to
\mu
):

\theta_{\mu}(B)=\frac{\mu(B)}{r(B)^{n}},

and its
\beta_{2,\mu}
coefficient:

\beta_{2,\mu}(B)=\inf_{L}\left(\frac{1}{r(B)^{n}}\int_{B}\left(\frac{{\rm dist}(x,L)}{r(B)}\right)^{2}\,d\mu(x)\right)^{1/2},

where the infimum is taken over all
n
-planes
L\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
and
r(B)
stands for the radius of
B
. For

B=B(x,r)
we may also write
\theta_{\mu}(x,r)
and
\beta_{2,\mu}(x,r)
instead of
\theta_{\mu}(B)
and

\beta_{2,\mu}(B)
. The coefficients
\beta_{2,\mu}
were introduced by David and Semmes in their fundamental works
[
DS1
]
,
[
DS2
]
on uniform rectifiability. They can be considered as
L^{2}
variants of
some analogous coefficients considered previously by Peter Jones in his celebrated travelling salesman theorem
[
Jo
]
.

The first main result of this paper is the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
satisfying the polynomial growth condition

(1.1)

\mu(B(x,r))\leq\theta_{0}\,r^{n}\quad\mbox{ for all $x\in\operatorname{supp}\mu$ and all $r>0$}

and such that
\|{\mathcal{R}}_{*}\mu\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}<\infty
.
Then

(1.2)

\int\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta_{2,\mu}(x,r)^{2}\,\theta_{\mu}(x,r)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\mu(x)\leq C\,(\big\|\operatorname{pv}{\mathcal{R}}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\theta_{0}^{2}\,\|\mu\|\big),

where
C
is an absolute constant.

Let us remark that the growth condition (
1.1
) and the assumption that
\|{\mathcal{R}}_{*}\mu\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}<\infty
ensures that
{\mathcal{R}}_{*}\mu(x)<\infty

\mu
-a.e., and in turn this implies the existence of principal values
\operatorname{pv}{\mathcal{R}}\mu(x)

\mu
-a.e., by
[
NToV2
]
, and so

\big\|\operatorname{pv}{\mathcal{R}}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
is well defined.

A converse to the estimate (
1.2
) also holds: if
\mu
satisfies the growth condition
(
1.1
), then

(1.3)

\|\operatorname{pv}{\mathcal{R}}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\leq C\,\int\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta_{2,\mu}(x,r)^{2}\,\theta_{\mu}(x,r)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\mu(x)+C\,\theta_{0}^{2}\,\|\mu\|,

where
C
is an absolute constant. This was shown in
[
AT
]
in the case
n=1
, and in
[
Gi
]
in full generality.

From (
1.3
), Theorem
1.1
, and a direct application of the
T1
theorem and Cotlar’s inequality for non-doubling measures (
[
NTrV1
]
,
[
NTrV2
]
) one deduces the following.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
with no point masses. Then
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
if and
only if it satisfies the polynomial growth condition

(1.4)

\mu(B(x,r))\leq C\,r^{n}\quad\mbox{ for all $x\in\operatorname{supp}\mu$ and all $r>0$}

and

(1.5)

\int_{B}\int_{0}^{r(B)}\beta_{2,\mu}(x,r)^{2}\,\theta_{\mu}(x,r)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\mu(x)\leq C^{2}\,\mu(B)\quad\mbox{ for any ball
$B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}$.}

Further, the optimal constant
C
is comparable to
\|{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}
.

In the case
n=1
the preceding results are already known. They were proven in
[
AT
]
, relying on the
corona decomposition involving the curvature of
\mu
from
[
To3
]
.
Further, for arbitrary
n>1
, it is also known that if one assumes that there exists a sufficiently large class of
L^{2}(\mu)
bounded singular integral operators with an odd Calderón-Zygmund kernel, then the condition (
1.5
)
holds, in any codimension (i.e., assuming that, instead of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, the ambient space is
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, with
d\geq n
). This was proved in
[
JNT
]
.

Observe that, when
\mu

is
n
-AD-regular,
\theta_{\mu}(x,r)\approx 1
for all
x\in\operatorname{supp}\mu
and
0<r\leq{\rm diam}(\operatorname{supp}\mu)
, and so
the condition (
1.5
) is equivalent to the uniform
n
-rectifiability of
\mu
, by
[
DS1
]
.
So one deduces that the
L^{2}(\mu)
boundedness
of
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}
implies the uniform
n
-rectifiability of
\mu
and then one recovers the solution of the David-Semmes
problem from
[
NToV1
]
. Recall that a measure
\mu
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is called
uniformly
n
-rectifiable
(UR) if it is
n
-AD-regular and
there exist constants
\kappa,M>0
such that for all
x\in\operatorname{supp}\mu
and all
0<r\leq{\rm diam}(\operatorname{supp}\mu)

there is a Lipschitz mapping
g
from the ball
B_{n}(0,r)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
\text{Lip}(g)\leq M
such that

\mu(B(x,r)\cap g(B_{n}(0,r)))\geq\kappa\,r^{n}.

It is worth comparing Theorem
1.2
with a related result obtained in
[
JNRT
]
in connection
with the fractional Riesz transform
{\mathcal{R}}^{s}
associated with the kernel

x/|x|^{s+1}
for
s\in(n,n+1)
. The precise result, which involves the
s
-dimensional density
\theta_{\mu}^{s}(x,r)=\mu(B(x,r))r^{-s}
, is the following.

Theorem

(
[
JNRT
]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
with no point masses and let
s\in(n,n+1)
. Then
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}^{s}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
if and
only if

(1.6)

\int_{B}\int_{0}^{r(B)}\theta^{s}_{\mu}(x,r)^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\mu(x)\leq C^{2}\,\mu(B)\quad\mbox{ for any ball
$B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}$.}

Further, the optimal constant
C
is comparable to
\|{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}^{s}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}
.

In the preceding theorem one does not need to ask any growth condition analogous to (
1.4
)
(with
n
interchanged with
s
) because this condition is already implied by (
1.6
). Observe that in
(
1.6
) the density
\theta^{s}_{\mu}(x,r)
replaces
\beta_{2,\mu}(x,r)^{2}
in (
1.5
), which scales similarly to

\theta^{s}(x,r)
when
n=s
. On the other hand, the proof of the last theorem in
[
JNRT
]
makes an
extensive use of blowup techniques, which essentially rely on the fact that any measure
\mu
satisfying the growth condition
\mu(B(x,r))\leq r^{s}
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
,
r>0
, and such that

{\mathcal{R}}^{s}\mu=0
in a suitable
BMO(\mu)
sense, must be the zero measure (see
[
JN1
]
,
[
JN2
]
). In
the codimension
1
case, one might expect that if
\mu
satisfies (
1.4
)
and
{\mathcal{R}}\mu=0
in the
BMO(\mu)
sense, then
\mu=c{\mathcal{H}}^{n}|_{L}
for some
n
-plane
L
. However, this is
still an open problem. If this were known to be true, probably in the present paper we
could use blowup arguments analogous to the ones in
[
JNRT
]
.

As shown in
[
AT
]
, the finiteness of the double integral on the left hand side of (
1.2
)
is equivalent to the existence of a suitable corona decomposition for
\mu
satisfying an appropriate packing
condition. This condition is stable by bilipschitz maps (see also
[
Gi
]
for more details). So we get the
following corollary.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
with no point masses. Let
{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
be a bilipschitz
map. Let
\sigma={\varphi}\#\mu
be the image measure of
\mu
by
{\varphi}
.
If
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
, then
{\mathcal{R}}_{\sigma}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\sigma)
. Further,

\|{\mathcal{R}}_{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(\sigma)\to L^{2}(\sigma)}\leq C\,\|{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)},

where
C
depends only on the bilipschitz constant of
{\varphi}
.

Remark that, up to now, the preceding result was not known even for the case of invertible affine maps such as the one defined by

{\varphi}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1})=(2x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n+1}).

As shown in
[
Gi
]
, the conditions (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) imply the
L^{2}(\mu)
boundedness of any singular integral operator of the form

T_{\mu}f(x)=\int K(x-y)\,f(y)\,d\mu(y),

where
K
is an odd kernel such that

(1.7)

|\nabla^{j}K(x)|\lesssim\frac{1}{|x|^{n+j}}\quad\mbox{ for all $x\neq 0$ and $0\leq j\leq 2$}

(remark that
T_{\mu}
is said to be bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
is the truncated operators
T_{\mu,{\varepsilon}}
, defined analogously to
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu,{\varepsilon}}
, are bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
uniformly on
{\varepsilon}>0
).
Then we deduce the following.

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
with no point masses. Let
T_{\mu}
be a singular integral
operator associated with an odd kernel
K
satisfying (
1.7
).
If
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
, then
T_{\mu}
is also bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
. Further,

\|T_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq C\,\|{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)},

where
C
depends just on
n
and the implicit constants in (
1.7
).

We turn now to the applications of the results above to Lipschitz harmonic functions and Lipschitz harmonic
capacity. Given a compact set
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, one says that
E
is removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions
if for any open set
\Omega\supset E
, any function
f:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
which is Lipschitz in
\Omega
and harmonic in

\Omega\setminus E

can be extended in a harmonic way to the whole
\Omega
. To study this problem and some related questions on approximation by Lipschitz harmonic functions it is useful to introduce the Lipschitz harmonic capacity
\kappa

(see
[
Pa
]
and
[
MP
]
). This is defined by

\kappa(E)=\sup|\langle\Delta f,1\rangle|,

where the supremum is taken over all Lipschitz functions
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
which are harmonic in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\setminus E
and satisfy
\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, with
\Delta f
understood in the sense of distributions. It turns
out that
E
is removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions if and only if
\kappa(E)=0
.

Extending previous results for analytic capacity from
[
To2
]
, Volberg showed in
[
Vo
]
that

\kappa(E)\approx\sup\mu(E),

where the supremum is taken over all measures
\mu
satisfying the polynomial growth condition
(
1.4
) with constant
C=1
and such that
\|{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq 1
.
Combining this result with Theorem
1.2
, we obtain:

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
be compact. Then

\kappa(E)\approx\mu(E),

where the supremum is taken over all Radon measures
\mu
such that

\mu(B(x,r))\leq r^{n}\quad\mbox{ for all $x\in\operatorname{supp}\mu$ and all $r>0$}

and

\int\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta_{2,\mu}(x,r)^{2}\,\theta_{\mu}(x,r)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\mu(x)\leq\mu(E).

To derive this corollary, remark that if
\mu
satisfies the conditions above, by Chebyshev one deduces that there is a big piece

F\subset E\cap\operatorname{supp}\mu
, with
\mu(F)\approx\mu(E)
, such that the measure
{\widetilde{\mu}}=\mu|_{F}
satisfies (
1.5
), and so

{\mathcal{R}}_{{\widetilde{\mu}}}
is bounded in
L^{2}({\widetilde{\mu}})
. Hence
\kappa(E)\gtrsim\mu(F)\approx\mu(E)
. The converse
direction of the corollary is a straightforward consequence of the aforementioned theorem of Volberg and
Theorem
1.2
.

As explained above, the conditions on the measure
\mu
in Corollary
1.5
are stable by bilipschitz maps. So we deduce that if
{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
is bilipschitz, then

\kappa(E)\approx\kappa({\varphi}(E))\quad\mbox{ for any compact set $E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}$,}

with the implicit constant just depending on the bilipschitz constant of
{\varphi}
and the ambient dimension.

Another suggestive characterization of the capacity
\kappa(E)
can be given in terms of the following potential,
which we call the Jones-Wolff potential of
\mu
:

U_{\mu}(x)=\sup_{r>0}\theta_{\mu}(x,r)+\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta_{2,\mu}(x,r)^{2}\,\theta_{\mu}(x,r)\,\frac{dr}{r}\right)^{1/2}.

Corollary 1.6
.

Let
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
be compact. Then

\kappa(E)\approx\sup\{\mu(E):\,U_{\mu}(x)\leq 1\,\forall x\in E\}.

An immediate consequence of this result is that
E
is non-removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions if and only if
it supports a non-zero measure such that
U_{\mu}(x)\leq 1
for all
x\in E
.

The characterization of the capacity
\kappa
and of removable sets for Lipschitz harmonic functions
in terms of a metric-geometric potential such as
U_{\mu}
should be considered as an analogue of the characterization
of analytic capacity and of removable sets for bounded analytic functions
in terms of curvature of measures
[
To2
]
. So one can think of the results stated in Corollaries

1.5
and
1.6
as possible solutions of the Painlevé problem for Lipschitz harmonic functions.

1.1.
Sketch of the proof

Next we will describe the main ideas involved in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, as well as the
main difficulties and innovations.

At the center of our proof is the notion of
cubes dominated from below
, which can be described as follows. For a Radon measure
\sigma
we consider the Wolff type
energy

\mathbb{E}(\sigma)=\int\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\sigma(B(x,r))}{r^{n-\frac{3}{8}}}\right)^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\sigma(x)=\int\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{\frac{3}{4}}\,\theta_{\sigma}(B(x,r))^{2}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\sigma(x).

Given the Radon measure
\mu
, we consider a suitably modified version of the David-Mattila lattice
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
associated
with
\mu
. Then for a given
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling cube
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
, we let
{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)
be a modified, discrete version of
\mathbb{E}(\mu|_{9Q})\,\ell(Q)^{-3/4}
.
We say that a
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling cube
Q
is
M
-dominated from below, and we write
Q\in{\mathsf{DB}}(M)
, if

{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)\geq M^{2}\,\Theta(Q)^{2}\,\mu(Q),

where
M\gg 1
is some fixed constant,
\Theta(Q)
is another discrete version of
\theta_{\mu}(2B_{Q})
, and
B_{Q}
is a ball concentric with
Q
, containing
Q
, with radius
comparable to
\ell(Q)
(for the precise definitions of
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling cubes,
\Theta(Q)
, and
{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)
see Section
3
). Essentially,
Q\in{\mathsf{DB}}(M)
means that there are many subcubes of
Q
which are not too small, and whose
\mu
-density is much larger than that of
Q
.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is divided into two main propositions. In Main Proposition
3.6
we prove the desired estimate up to an error term involving cubes dominated from below:

(1.8)

\int\!\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta_{2,\mu}(x,r)^{2}\,\theta_{\mu}(x,r)\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\mu(x)\leq C(M)\,\Big(\|{\mathcal{R}}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\theta_{0}^{2}\,\|\mu\|+\sum_{Q\in{\mathsf{DB}}(M)}{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)\Big).

In Main Proposition
3.7
we prove that if the
{\mathsf{DB}}
-parameter
M
is larger than some dimensional constant
M_{0}
, then we may estimate the error term:

(1.9)

\sum_{Q\in{\mathsf{DB}}(M)}{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)\leq C\,\big(\|{\mathcal{R}}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\theta_{0}^{2}\,\|\mu\|\big).

Clearly, these two estimates give Theorem
1.1
.

Originally, Main Proposition
3.6
was proved in
[
DT
]
, while Main Proposition
3.7
was proved in
[
To6
]
. In this article we merge the two proofs, making the whole argument more streamlined and significantly shorter.
1
1

1

Roughly speaking, Sections
2
–
5
and
7
–
10
come from
[
DT
]
, while Sections
6
and
11
–
13
come from
[
To6
]
.
Our proof is divided into 4 parts.

Part 1
, which consists of Sections
2
and
3
, contains various preliminaries, including an enhanced version of the dyadic lattice of David and Mattila.

In
Part 2
, which includes Sections
4
,
5
, and
6
, we prove results which are eventually used in the proofs of both (
1.8
) and (
1.9
). Roughly speaking, showing either (
1.8
) or (
1.9
) involves proving the following implication: “if the density of
\mu
oscillates at certain scales and locations, then the corresponding Haar coefficients of
{\mathcal{R}}\mu
are large”. In Part 2 we prove this implication.

First, in Section
4
we conduct a stopping time argument involving
{\mathsf{HD}}
(high density),
{\mathsf{LD}}
(low density), and
{\mathsf{OP}}
(“optional”) stopping time conditions (the optional condition is assumed to be void in the proof of (
1.8
), whereas in the proof of (
1.9
) the optional condition involves the
{\mathsf{DB}}
-family). We say that a cube
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
has
moderate decrement of Wolff energy
, denoted by
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
, if
R
contains quite many
{\mathsf{HD}}
stopping cubes. To each
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
we associate certain extended tree
{\mathcal{T}}
(obtained by running the stopping time argument again), and we say that the extended tree is
tractable
, denoted by
R\in{\mathsf{Trc}}
, if the density of many cubes in some intermediate generations increases (this is ensured by the
{\mathsf{MDW}}
condition), and later in many stopping cubes the density decreases. In other words, for
R\in{\mathsf{Trc}}
the
\mu
-density oscillates a lot at many scales and locations of the extended tree associated to
R
. As it turns out, to each cube in
{\mathsf{MDW}}
one may associate a family of tractable trees, and so we can concentrate on the tractable trees.

Given a tractable tree
{\mathcal{T}}
, in Section
5
we construct a measure
\eta
that approximates the measure
\mu
at the level of some regularized stopping cubes of
{\mathcal{T}}
. Then, we use a variational argument to obtain good lower bounds for
\|{\mathcal{R}}\eta\|_{L^{2}(\eta)}
. These bounds are transferred to
{\mathcal{R}}\mu
(i.e., to the Haar coefficients of
{\mathcal{R}}\mu
for the cubes in the tree) in Section
6
. The idea of applying a variational argument like this originates from the work
[
ENV
]
by Eiderman, Nazarov, and Volberg and the reduction to the tractable trees comes from the work
[
RT
]
by Reguera and the second author of this paper. The article
[
JNRT
]
includes an improved version of that variational argument. Unlike the present paper,
[
JNRT
]
makes an extensive use of compactness arguments, which do not work so well in our situation, where the geometry plays a more important role.

The implementation of the variational argument and the transference of the estimates from the approximating measure
\eta
to
\mu
is more difficult in the present
paper than in
[
RT
]
or in other related works such as
[
JNRT
]
. Some of the
difficulties arise from the fact that, for technical reasons (essentially, we need that many cubes of
the intermediate generations with high density are located far from the boundary of the root of the tree), we have to consider trees of “enlarged cubes”. This causes an overlapping between different trees that
has to be quantified carefully (this is done in Sections
8
and
12
).
On the other hand, the transference of the lower estimate for
\|{\mathcal{R}}\eta\|_{L^{2}(\eta)}

to the Haar coefficients of
{\mathcal{R}}\mu
for the cubes in the tree originates many error terms. Roughly speaking, in order to be able to transfer that lower bound for
\|{\mathcal{R}}\eta\|_{L^{2}(\eta)}
to
\mu
we need
the error terms to be smaller than the lower bound of
\|{\mathcal{R}}\eta\|_{L^{2}(\eta)}
. Some of these
error terms are difficult to handle and we only can bound them in terms of the
energies
{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)
, which are problematic in case
Q\in{\mathsf{DB}}
. For this to work, we need
an enhanced version of the dyadic lattice of David and Mattila that is obtained in Section

2
. This is an essential tool for our arguments. Furthermore, we can only quantify the presence of cubes from
{\mathsf{DB}}
in most of the trees
{\mathcal{T}}
by a bootstrapping argument which gives rather weak bounds.

In
Part 3
, spanning Sections
7
–
10
, we prove (
1.8
). In Section
7
we perform a corona decomposition of the dyadic lattice
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
into trees of cubes where the density of the cubes does not oscillate too much. We call the family of roots of these trees
{\mathsf{Top}}
. Then, roughly speaking, in each tree the measure

\mu
behaves as if it were AD-regular, and from the
L^{2}(\mu)
boundedness of
{\mathcal{R}}_{\mu}
and

[
NToV1
]
, one should expect that
\mu
is close to some uniformly rectifiable measure at the locations and scales of the cubes in the tree, so that one can obtain a good packing condition
for the
\beta_{\mu,2}
coefficients of the cubes in the tree.
For this strategy to work, we need to show that the family
{\mathsf{Top}}
satisfies a suitable packing condition. This is done in Sections
8
and
9
, and the proof relies on the results from Part 2.
The last stage of the proof of (
1.8
) consists of estimating the

\beta_{\mu,2}
coefficients in each tree where the density does not oscillate too much.
This step, which requires a delicate approximation by an AD-regular measure which has its own interest,
is performed in Section
10
.

In
Part 4
, containing Sections
11
–
13
, we prove (
1.9
). The first step is the construction of a
good dominating family

{\mathsf{GDF}}
, closely related to
{\mathsf{DB}}
. It consists of cubes
Q
which, in a sense, contain many stopping cubes whose density is much larger than
the density
\Theta(Q)
(in particular,
{\mathsf{GDF}}\subset{\mathsf{MDW}}
). The selection of this family, in Section
11
, is one of the key steps for the proof of (
1.9
). In Section
12
we quantify the overlaps between the tractable trees associated to different

R\in{\mathsf{GDF}}
. At this point the optional stopping time condition
{\mathsf{OP}}
from Section
4
is used. Finally, in Section
13
the proof of (
1.9
) is concluded, relying on the estimates obtained in Part 2, which are applied to the tractable trees associated to the cubes from
{\mathsf{GDF}}
.

In the whole paper we denote by
C
or
c
some constants that may depend on the dimension and perhaps other fixed parameters. Their values may change at different occurrences. On the contrary, constants with subscripts, like
C_{0}
, retain their values.
For
a,b\geq 0
, we write
a\lesssim b
if there is
C>0
such that
a\leq Cb
. We write
a\approx b
to mean
a\lesssim b\lesssim a
.

Part 1: Preliminaries

2.
The modified dyadic lattice of David and Mattila

In this section we introduce the dyadic lattice of cubes
with small boundaries of David-Mattila
[
DM
]
associated with a Radon measure
\mu
. The properties of the lattice are summarized in the next lemma. Later on we will show how its construction
can be modified in order to obtain additional properties relevant for our arguments.

Lemma 2.1

(David, Mattila)
.

Let
\mu
be a compactly supported Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Consider two constants
C_{0}>1
and
A_{0}>5000\,C_{0}
and denote
E=\operatorname{supp}\mu
.
Then there exists a sequence of partitions of
E
into
Borel subsets
Q
,
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
, with the following properties:

•

For each integer
k\geq 0
,
E
is the disjoint union of the “cubes”
Q
,
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
, and
if
k<l
,
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,l}
, and
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
, then either
Q\cap R=\varnothing
or else
Q\subset R
.

•

The general position of the cubes
Q
can be described as follows. For each
k\geq 0
and each cube
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
, there is a ball
B(Q)=B(x_{Q},r(Q))
such that

x_{Q}\in E,\qquad A_{0}^{-k}\leq r(Q)\leq C_{0}\,A_{0}^{-k},

E\cap B(Q)\subset Q\subset E\cap 28\,B(Q)=E\cap B(x_{Q},28r(Q)),

and

the balls
5B(Q)
,
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
, are disjoint.

•

The cubes
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
have small boundaries. That is, for each
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
and each
integer
l\geq 0
, set

N_{l}^{ext}(Q)=\{x\in E\setminus Q:\,{\rm dist}(x,Q)<A_{0}^{-k-l}\},

N_{l}^{int}(Q)=\{x\in Q:\,{\rm dist}(x,E\setminus Q)<A_{0}^{-k-l}\},

and

N_{l}(Q)=N_{l}^{ext}(Q)\cup N_{l}^{int}(Q).

Then

(2.1)

\mu(N_{l}(Q))\leq(C^{-1}C_{0}^{-3d-1}A_{0})^{-l}\,\mu(90B(Q)).

•

Denote by
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}^{db}
the family of cubes
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
for which

(2.2)

\mu(100B(Q))\leq C_{0}\,\mu(B(Q)).

We have that
r(Q)=A_{0}^{-k}
when
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}^{db}

and

(2.3)

\mu(100B(Q))\leq C_{0}^{-l}\,\mu(100^{l+1}B(Q))\quad\mbox{for all $l\geq 1$ with $100^{l}\leq C_{0}$ and $Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}^{db}$.}

Remark 2.2
.

We choose the constants
C_{0}
and
A_{0}
so that

A_{0}=C_{0}^{C(d)},

where
C(d)
depends
just on
d
and
C_{0}
is big enough.

We use the notation
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
. Observe that the families
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
are only defined for
k\geq 0
. So the diameters of the cubes from
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
are uniformly
bounded from above.
We set

\ell(Q)=56\,C_{0}\,A_{0}^{-k}
and we call it the side length of
Q
. Notice that

C_{0}^{-1}\ell(Q)\leq{\rm diam}(28B(Q))\leq\ell(Q).

Observe that
r(Q)\approx{\rm diam}(Q)\approx\ell(Q)
.
Also we call
x_{Q}
the center of
Q
, and the cube
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k-1}
such that
Q^{\prime}\supset Q
the parent of
Q
.
We denote the family of cubes from
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k+1}
which are contained in
Q
by
{\mathcal{C}h}(Q)
, and we call their elements children or sons of
Q
.
We set

B_{Q}=28B(Q)=B(x_{Q},28\,r(Q))
, so that

E\cap\tfrac{1}{28}B_{Q}\subset Q\subset B_{Q}\subset B(x_{Q},\ell(Q)/2).

For a given
\gamma\in(0,1)
, let
A_{0}
be big enough so that the constant
C^{-1}C_{0}^{-3d-1}A_{0}
in
(
2.1
) satisfies

C^{-1}C_{0}^{-3d-1}A_{0}>A_{0}^{\gamma}>10.

Then we deduce that, for all
0<\lambda\leq 1
,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mu\bigl(\{x\in Q:{\rm dist}(x,E\setminus Q)\leq\lambda\,\ell(Q)\}\bigr)+\mu\bigl(\bigl\{x\in 3.5B_{Q}\setminus Q:{\rm dist}

\displaystyle(x,Q)\leq\lambda\,\ell(Q)\}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq_{\gamma}c\,\lambda^{\gamma}\,\mu(3.5B_{Q}).

We denote

{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}^{db}
.
Note that, in particular, from (
2.2
) it follows that

(2.5)

\mu(3B_{Q})\leq\mu(100B(Q))\leq C_{0}\,\mu(Q)\qquad\mbox{if $Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}.$}

For this reason we will call the cubes from
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}
doubling.
Given
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
, we denote by
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(Q)

the family of cubes from
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
which are contained in
Q
. Analogously,
we write
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}(Q)={\mathcal{D}}^{db}_{\mu}\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(Q)
.

As shown in
[
DM
, Lemma 5.28]
, every cube
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
can be covered
\mu
-a.e. by a family of doubling cubes:

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
. Suppose that the constants
A_{0}
and
C_{0}
in Lemma
2.1
are
chosen as in Remark
2.2
. Then there exists a family of
doubling cubes
\{Q_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subset{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}
, with

Q_{i}\subset R
for all
i
, such that their union covers
\mu
-almost all
R
.

The following result is proved in
[
DM
, Lemma 5.31]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
k\leq j
,
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
and
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(R)
be a cube such that all the intermediate cubes
S
,

Q\subsetneq S\subsetneq R
are non-doubling (i.e. belong to
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}
).
Suppose that the constants
A_{0}
and
C_{0}
in Lemma
2.1
are
chosen as in Remark
2.2
.
Then

(2.6)

\mu(100B(Q))\leq A_{0}^{-10d(j-k-1)}\mu(100B(R)).

From this lemma we deduce:

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
Q,R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
be as in Lemma
2.4
.
Then

\theta_{\mu}(100B(Q))\leq(C_{0}A_{0})^{n+1}\,A_{0}^{-9d(j-k-1)}\,\theta_{\mu}(100B(R))

and

\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:Q\subset S\subset R}\theta_{\mu}(100B(S))\leq c\,\theta_{\mu}(100B(R)),

with
c
depending on
C_{0}
and
A_{0}
.

For the easy proof, see

[
To4
, Lemma 4.4]
, for example.

For
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
and
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
we define

(2.7)

\Delta_{Q}f=\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{C}h}(Q)}m_{\mu,S}(f)\chi_{S}-m_{\mu,Q}(f)\chi_{Q},

where
m_{\mu,S}(f)
stands for the average of
f
on
S
with respect to
\mu
.
Then we have the orthogonal expansion, for any cube
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
,

\chi_{R}\bigl(f-m_{\mu,R}(f)\bigr)=\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(R)}\Delta_{Q}f,

in the
L^{2}(\mu)
-sense, so that

\|\chi_{R}\bigl(f-m_{\mu,R}(f)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}=\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(R)}\|\Delta_{Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

In this paper we will have to estimate terms such as
\|{\mathcal{R}}(\chi_{Q}\mu)\|_{L^{2}(\mu{\lfloor}_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q})}
,
which leads to dealing with integrals of the form

\int_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q}\left(\int_{Q}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\,d\mu(y)\right)^{2}d\mu(x).

Our next objective is to show that integrals such as this one can be estimated in terms of the Wolff type energy
{\mathcal{E}}(2Q)
, to be defined soon.

We need some additional notation.
Given
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
and
\lambda>1
, we denote by
\lambda Q
the union of cubes
P
from the same
generation as
Q
such that
{\rm dist}(x_{Q},P)\leq\lambda\,\ell(Q)
. Notice that

(2.8)

\lambda Q\subset B(x_{Q},(\lambda+\tfrac{1}{2})\ell(Q)).

Also, we let

{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(\lambda Q)=\{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:P\subset\lambda Q,\,\ell(P)\leq\ell(Q)\},

and, for
k\geq 0
,

{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}(\lambda Q)=\{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:P\subset\lambda Q,\,\ell(P)=A_{0}^{-k}\ell(Q)\},\qquad{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{k}(\lambda Q)=\bigcup_{j\geq k}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}(\lambda Q).

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mu
be a compactly supported Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Assume that
\mu
has polynomial growth of degree
n
and let
\gamma\in(0,1)
. The lattice
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
from Lemma

2.1
can be constructed so that the following holds for all
all
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\int_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q}\left(\int_{Q}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\,d\mu(y)\right)^{2}d\mu(x)+

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\left(\int_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\,d\mu(y)\right)^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq C(\gamma)\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:P\subset 2Q}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\mu(P).

Remark that the polynomial growth assumption is just necessary to ensure that some of the integrals above are finite. In fact, the constant
C(\gamma)
does not depend on the polynomial growth constant.

To prove the lemma, we denote

(2.9)

{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{int}(Q)=\big\{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(Q):2B_{P}\cap(\operatorname{supp}\mu\setminus Q)\neq\varnothing\big\}

and

(2.10)

{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{ext}(Q)=\big\{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:\ell(P)\leq\ell(Q),P\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\setminus Q,\,2B_{P}\cap Q\neq\varnothing\big\}.

Also,

(2.11)

{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}(Q)={\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{int}(Q)\cup{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{ext}(Q),

and, for
k\geq 0
,

{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,k}(Q)=\{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}:\ell(P)=A_{0}^{-k}\ell(Q)\}.

We need some auxiliary results. The first one is the following.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mu
be a compactly supported Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
. For any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we have

(2.12)

\displaystyle\int_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q}\left(\int_{Q}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\,d\mu(y)\right)^{2}d\mu(x)\,+

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\left(\int_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\,d\mu(y)\right)^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha,A_{0}}\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}(Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(P)}\right)^{\alpha}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P).

Proof.

Observe that, for
x\in 2B_{Q}\setminus Q
,

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{y\in Q:|x-y|\geq r(B_{Q})/2}+\int_{y\in Q:|x-y|<r(B_{Q})/2}\right)\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{A_{0}}\frac{\mu(Q)}{r(B_{Q})^{n}}+\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{ext}(Q):x\in P}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P}).

Thus,

\int_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q}\left(\int_{Q}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\,d\mu(y)\right)^{2}d\mu(x)\\
\lesssim_{A_{0}}\left(\frac{\mu(Q)}{r(B_{Q})^{n}}\right)^{2}\,\mu(2B_{Q})+\int_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q}\bigg(\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{ext}(Q):x\in P}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})\bigg)^{2}d\mu(x).

By Hölder’s inequality, for any
\alpha>0
,

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{ext}(Q):x\in P}

\displaystyle\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{ext}(Q):x\in P}\left(\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(P)}\right)^{\alpha}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{ext}(Q):x\in P}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\alpha}\bigg).

The last sum above is bounded above by

\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:x\in P,P\subset 2Q}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\alpha}\leq C(\alpha).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{Q}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\,d\mu(y)\right)^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha,A_{0}}\frac{\mu(Q)^{2}\,\mu(2B_{Q})}{r(B_{Q})^{2n}}+\int_{2B_{Q}\setminus Q}\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{ext}(Q):x\in P}\left(\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(P)}\right)^{\alpha}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\theta_{\mu}(2B_{Q})^{2}\,\mu(Q)+\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}^{ext}(Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(P)}\right)^{\alpha}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P).

The estimate of the second integral on the left hand side of (
2.12
) is analogous.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\mu
be a compactly supported Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and let
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
Assume that
\mu
has polynomial growth of degree
n
and let
\gamma\in(0,1)
. The lattice
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
from Lemma

2.1
can be constructed so that the following holds for all
all
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
:

(2.13)

\sum_{S\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,1}(Q)}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(2S)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)\lesssim C_{0}^{6d+1}A_{0}^{-1}\!\!\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{1}(2Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P).

Proof.

We will describe the relevant changes required in the arguments in
[
DM
, Theorem 3.2]
in order to get the estimate (
2.13
). We will
use the same notation as in that theorem, with the exception of the constant
A
in
[
DM
, Theorem 3.2]
, which here we denote by
A_{0}
.

Denote
E=\operatorname{supp}\mu
.
For each generation
k\geq 0
, the starting point to construct
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}

consists of choosing, for each
x\in E
, a suitable radius
r^{k}(x)
such that

(2.14)

A_{0}^{-k}\leq r^{k}(x)\leq C_{0}A_{0}^{-k}

depending on the doubling properties of the ball
B(x,r^{k}(x))
(see
[
DM
, (3.17)-(3.20)]
).
Next, one chooses two auxiliary radii
r_{1}^{k}(x)
and
r_{2}^{k}(x)
such that

\frac{11}{10}\,r^{k}(x)<r_{1}^{k}(x)<\frac{12}{10}\,r^{k}(x),

25\,r^{k}(x)<r_{2}^{k}(x)<26\,r^{k}(x),

and such that the following small boundary conditions hold:

(2.15)

\mu\big(\big\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\!:{\rm dist}(y,\partial B(x,r_{1}^{k}(x)))\leq\tau\,r^{k}(x)\big\}\big)\leq C\tau\,\mu\big(B(x,\tfrac{13}{10}r^{k}(x))\big)\quad\mbox{for $0<\tau<\tfrac{1}{10}$,}

and

(2.16)

\mu\big(\big\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\!:{\rm dist}(y,\partial B(x,r_{2}^{k}(x)))\leq\tau\,r^{k}(x)\big\}\big)\leq C\tau\,\mu\big(B(x,27r^{k}(x))\big)\quad\mbox{for $0<\tau<1$.}

At this point we will require the auxiliary radii
r_{1}^{k}(x)
and
r_{2}^{k}(x)
to be chosen so that an additional condition holds. Set
A(x,r,R)=B(x,R)\setminus B(x,r).

Observe first that

(2.17)

\displaystyle\int_{\tfrac{11}{10}r^{k}(x)}^{\tfrac{12}{10}r^{k}(x)}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}\int_{A(x,\,t-300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1},\,t+300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1})}\theta_{\mu}(y,112C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-j-1})^{2}\,d\mu(y)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}\int_{B(x,\tfrac{12}{10}r^{k}(x)+300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1})}\theta_{\mu}(y,112C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-j-1})^{2}\,{\mathcal{L}}^{1}(I_{x,y,k})\,d\mu(y),

where we applied Fubini and we denoted by
I_{x,y,k}
the interval

\displaystyle I_{x,y,k}

\displaystyle=\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}:t-300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1}\leq|x-y|\leq t+300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1}\}

\displaystyle=\big[|x-y|-300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1},|x-y|+300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1}\big].

Obviously, its Lebesgue measure is
{\mathcal{L}}^{1}(I_{x,y,k})=600C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1}
, and so the left hand side of (
2.17
)
is bounded above by

600\,C_{0}\,A_{0}^{-k-1}\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}\!\!\int_{B(x,\tfrac{13}{10}r^{k}(x))}\theta_{\mu}(y,112C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-j-1})^{2}\,d\mu(y).

Thus, by Chebyshev, the set
U_{1}^{k}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
of those
t\in[\tfrac{11}{10}r^{k}(x),\tfrac{12}{10}r^{k}(x)]
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}

\displaystyle\int_{A(x,t-300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1},t+300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1})}\theta_{\mu}(y,112C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-j-1})^{2}\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle>\frac{10^{5}\,C_{0}\,A_{0}^{-k-1}}{r^{k}(x)}\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}\int_{B(x,\tfrac{13}{10}r^{k}(x))}\theta_{\mu}(y,112C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-j-1})^{2}\,d\mu(y)

satisfies

|U_{1}^{k}|\leq\frac{1}{100}\,r^{k}(x).

By a standard argument involving the boundedness of the maximal Hardy-Littlewood operator
from
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
to
L^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
, one can deduce that there exists some

r_{1}^{k}(x)\in[\tfrac{11}{10}r^{k}(x),\tfrac{12}{10}r^{k}(x)]\setminus U_{1}^{k}

such that (
2.15
) holds. The fact that
r_{1}^{k}(x)\not\in\ U_{1}^{k}

ensures that

(2.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}

\displaystyle\int_{A(x,r_{1}^{k}(x)-300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1},r_{1}^{k}(x)+300C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1})}\theta_{\mu}(y,112C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-j-1})^{2}\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{10^{5}\,C_{0}\,A_{0}^{-k-1}}{r^{k}(x)}\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}\int_{B(x,\tfrac{13}{10}r^{k}(x))}\theta_{\mu}(y,112C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-j-1})^{2}\,d\mu(y).

An analogous argument shows that

r^{k}_{2}(x)
can be taken such that, besides (
2.16
), the
preceding estimate also holds with
r_{1}^{k}(x)
replaced
by
r_{2}^{k}(x)
and
B(x,\tfrac{13}{10}r^{k}(x))
replaced by
B(x,27r^{k}(x))
.

As in
[
DM
, Theorem 3.2]
, we denote
B_{1}^{k}(x)=B(x,r_{1}^{k}(x))
and
B_{2}^{k}(x)=B(x,r_{2}^{k}(x))
, and by a Vitali type covering lemma we select a family of points
x\in I^{k}

such that the balls
\{B(x,5r^{k}(x))\}_{x\in I^{k}}
are disjoint, while the balls
\{B(x,25r^{k}(x))\}_{x\in I^{k}}
cover
E
. We also denote

B_{3}^{k}(x)=B_{2}^{k}(x)\setminus\bigg(\bigcup_{y\in I^{k}\setminus\{x\}}B_{1}^{k}(y)\bigg).

For
x\in I_{k}
, let
J(x)
be the family of those

y\in I^{k}\setminus\{x\}
such that
B_{1}^{k}(y)\cap B_{2}^{k}(x)\neq\varnothing
. As explained in
[
DM
, Theorem 3.2]
, using (
2.14
) it is easy to check

\#J(x)\leq CC_{0}^{d}
.

Next we consider an order in
I^{k}
such that

y<x
in
I^{k}
whenever
\mu(B(x,90r^{k}(x)))<\mu(B(y,90r^{k}(y)))

and we define

B_{4}^{k}(x)=B_{3}^{k}(x)\setminus\bigg(\bigcup_{y\in I^{k}:y<x}B_{3}^{k}(y)\bigg).

Again, as explained in
[
DM
, Theorem 3.2]
, using (
2.14
) it is easy to check
that, for each
x\in I^{k}
, there are at most
CC_{0}^{n+1}
sets
B_{3}^{k}(y)
that intersect
B_{3}^{k}(x)
, with

y\in I^{k}
.

The family
\{B_{4}^{k}(x)\}_{x\in I^{k}}
is a first approximation to
\{Q\}_{Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{k}}
.
Indeed, by the arguments in
[
DM
, Theorem 3.2]
, for each
x\in I^{k}
one constructs
a set
Q^{k}(x)\subset E

such that, denoting

{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}=\{Q^{k}(x)\}_{x\in I^{k}},

the properties stated in Lemma
2.1
hold, with
r(Q^{k}(x))=r^{k}(x)
and
B(Q^{k}(x))=B(x,r^{k}(x))
.
In particular,

B(x,r^{k}(x))\cap E\subset Q^{k}(x)\subset B(x,28r^{k}(x)).

Also, as shown in
[
DM
, (3.61)]
, it holds

(2.19)

{\rm dist}(y,\partial B_{4}^{k}(x))\leq 51C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1}\quad\mbox{ for all $y\in N_{1}(Q(x))$.}

For a cube
Q=Q^{k}(x)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
, we write
r(Q)=r^{k}(x)
,
B(Q)=B(x,r^{k}(x))
and

B_{i}(Q)=B_{i}^{k}(x)
for
i=1,\ldots,4
.
By an argument quite similar to the one used in
[
DM
, Theorem 3.2]
to prove (
2.19
), we will show now that

(2.20)

2S\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{5A_{0}^{-1}\ell(Q)}(\partial B_{4}(Q))\quad\mbox{ for any $S\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,1}(Q)$,}

where
{\mathcal{U}}_{\ell}(A)
stands for the
\ell
-neighborhood of
A
.
This will be needed below to prove
(
2.13
). The condition
S\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,1}(Q)
tells us that either
S\subset Q
and

2B_{S}\cap(E\setminus Q)\neq\varnothing
, or
S\subset E\setminus Q
and

2B_{S}\cap Q\neq\varnothing
. Assume the first option (the arguments for the second one are analogous). So there exists some point
z\in E\setminus Q
such that
|x_{S}-z|\leq 2r(B_{S})=56r(S)
.
Let
x\in I^{k}
be such that
z\in Q^{k}(x)
. Then we have
{\rm dist}(z,B_{4}^{k}(x))\leq 50C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1}

and also
{\rm dist}(x_{S},B_{4}^{k}(x_{Q}))\leq 50C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1}
, by

[
DM
, (3.50)]
(see also the first paragraph after
[
DM
, (3.61)]
).
Since the sets
B_{4}^{k}(x_{Q})
,
B_{4}^{k}(x)
are disjoint, we deduce that

{\rm dist}(x_{S},\partial B_{4}^{k}(x))\leq 50C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1}+56\,r(S)\leq 106C_{0}A_{0}^{-k-1}<2A_{0}^{-1}\ell(Q).

Together with the fact that
2S\subset B\big(x_{S},\tfrac{5}{2}\ell(S)\big)
, this gives (
2.20
).

Notice that, for each
j\geq 0
,

\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,j}(2S)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)\lesssim C_{0}^{2n}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}\int_{2S}\theta_{\mu}(x,2A_{0}^{-j}\ell(S))^{2}\,d\mu(x).

Then we obtain

(2.21)

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,1}(Q)}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(2S)}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{0}^{2n}\sum_{S\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,1}(Q)}\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}\int_{2S}\theta_{\mu}(x,2A_{0}^{-j}\ell(S))^{2}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{0}^{2n+d}\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}\int_{{\mathcal{U}}_{5A_{0}^{-1}\ell(Q)}(\partial B_{4}(Q))}\theta_{\mu}(x,2A_{0}^{-j-1}\ell(Q))^{2}\,d\mu(x).

Denote by
{\widetilde{J}}(Q)
the family of cubes
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
such that
B_{2}(R)\cap B_{4}(Q)\neq\varnothing
, so that, by the above construction we have

\partial B_{4}(Q)\subset\bigcup_{R\in{\widetilde{J}}(Q)}(\partial B_{1}(R)\cup\partial B_{2}(R)).

Also, notice that
\#{\widetilde{J}}(Q)\leq C\,C_{0}^{d}
.
From (
2.18
) we deduce that, for each
R\in{\widetilde{J}}(Q)
and
i=1,2
,

(2.22)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{U}}_{5A_{0}^{-1}\ell(R)}(\partial B_{i}(R))}\theta_{\mu}(x,2A_{0}^{-j-1}\ell(R))^{2}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq C\,C_{0}A_{0}^{-1}\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{0}^{-\gamma(j+1)}\int_{27B(R)}\theta_{\mu}(x,2A_{0}^{-j-1}\ell(R))^{2}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq C\,C_{0}A_{0}^{-1}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{k+1}:P\cap 27B(R)\neq\varnothing}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\,\theta_{\mu}(x_{P},3\ell(P))^{2}\,\mu(P),

Notice that for
Q,R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
as above, the condition
B_{2}(R)\cap B_{4}(Q)\neq\varnothing
implies that
26B(Q)\cap 26B(R)\neq\varnothing
. Then, if

P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{k+1}
is such that
P\cap 27B(R)\neq\varnothing
, we derive

{\rm dist}(x_{Q},P)\leq|x_{Q}-x_{R}|+27r(R)\leq 26(r(Q)+r(R))+27r(R)\leq 79C_{0}A_{0}^{-k}=\frac{79}{56}\,\ell(Q).

Then, since
\ell(P)\leq A_{0}^{-1}\ell(Q)
, we infer that

(2.23)

B(x_{P},3\ell(P))\cap\operatorname{supp}\mu\subset 2Q.

Also, we can write

\displaystyle\theta_{\mu}(x_{P},3\ell(P))^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\mu(B(x_{P},3\ell(P)))^{3}}{\ell(P)^{2n}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\ell(P)^{2n}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:\ell(P^{\prime})=\ell(P),\\
P^{\prime}\cap B(x_{P},3\ell(P))\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu(P^{\prime})\bigg)^{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{0}^{2d}\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:\ell(P^{\prime})=\ell(P),\\
P^{\prime}\cap B(x_{P},3\ell(P))\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}\!\frac{\mu(P^{\prime})^{3}}{\ell(P^{\prime})^{2n}}\lesssim C_{0}^{2d}\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:\ell(P^{\prime})=\ell(P),\\
P^{\prime}\cap B(x_{P},3\ell(P))\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}\!\!\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P^{\prime}})^{2}\,\mu(P^{\prime}),

where we used the fact that the sums above are only over
CC_{0}^{d}
terms at most.
Together with (
2.23
), this implies that
the right hand side of (
2.22
) does not exceed

C\,C_{0}^{2d+1}A_{0}^{-1}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{k+1}:P\cap 27B(R)\neq\varnothing}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(2Q):\ell(P^{\prime})=\ell(P),\\
P^{\prime}\cap B(x_{P},3\ell(P))\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P^{\prime}})^{2}\,\mu(P^{\prime})\\
\leq C\,C_{0}^{3d+1}A_{0}^{-1}\sum_{P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{1}(2Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P^{\prime})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P^{\prime}})^{2}\,\mu(P^{\prime}).

By this estimate and (
2.21
), summing over all
R\in{\widetilde{J}}(Q)
, we get

\sum_{S\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,1}(Q)}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(2S)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)\lesssim C_{0}^{6d+1}A_{0}^{-1}\!\!\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{1}(2Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P),

as wished.
∎

By Lemma
2.7
, it is clear that to complete the proof of Lemma
2.6
it suffices to show the following result.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\mu
be a compactly supported Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Assume that
\mu
has polynomial growth of degree
n
and let
\gamma\in(0,1)
. The lattice
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
from Lemma
2.1
can be constructed so that the following holds for all
all
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
:

(2.24)

\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}(Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(P)}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)\lesssim_{A_{0},\gamma}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:P\subset 2Q}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\mu(P).

Proof.

To prove (
2.24
) notice that, for each
k\geq 1
, by Lemma
2.8
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,k}(Q)}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{1}(2R)}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{R\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,k-1}(Q)}\sum_{S\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,1}(R)}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(2S)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(S)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle=A_{0}^{\gamma}\sum_{R\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,k-1}(Q)}\sum_{S\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,1}(R)}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(2S)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\leq CC_{0}^{6d+1}A_{0}^{\gamma-1}\sum_{R\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,k-1}(Q)}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{1}(2R)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)}\right)^{\gamma}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P).

Iterating the preceding estimate, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,k+1}(Q)}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{R\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,k}(Q)}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,1}(2R)}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}^{\gamma}\sum_{R\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,k}(Q)}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{1}(2R)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)}\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}^{\gamma}\,(CC_{0}^{6d+1}A_{0}^{\gamma-1})^{k}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{1}(2Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu}(Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(P)}\right)^{(1-\gamma)/2}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\theta_{\mu}(2B_{Q})^{2}\,\mu(Q)+\sum_{k\geq 1}A_{0}^{(1-\gamma)k/2}\sum_{P\in{\widetilde{{\mathcal{D}}}}_{\mu,k}(Q)}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\theta_{\mu}(2B_{Q})^{2}\,\mu(Q)+A_{0}^{\gamma}\sum_{k\geq 1}A_{0}^{(1-\gamma)k/2}(CC_{0}^{6d+1}A_{0}^{\gamma-1})^{k-1}\!\!\!\!\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{1}(2Q)}\!\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim_{A_{0},\gamma}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(2Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\gamma}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P),

taking
A_{0}
big enough for the last estimate. This yields (
2.24
).
∎

3.
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling cubes, cubes dominated from below, and the Main Propositions

In the rest of the paper we assume that
\mu
is a compactly supported Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
with polynomial
growth of degree
n
and
that
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
is a David-Mattila dyadic lattice satisfying the properties
described in the preceding section, in particular, the ones in Lemmas
2.1

and
2.6
, with
\gamma=9/10
.
By rescaling, we assume that
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
is defined for all
k\geq k_{0}
, with
A_{0}^{-k_{0}}\approx{\rm diam}(\operatorname{supp}\mu)
, and we also assume that there is a unique cube in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k_{0}}
which coincides
with the whole
\operatorname{supp}\mu
.
Further, from now on, we allow all the constants
C
and all implicit constants in the relations
“
\lesssim
”, “
\approx
", to depend on the parameters
C_{0},A_{0}
of the dyadic lattice of David-Mattila.

3.1.
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling cubes and the family
{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)

We denote

{\mathcal{P}}(Q)=\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset Q}\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)^{n+1}}\,\mu(2B_{R}).

We say that a cube
Q
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling if

{\mathcal{P}}(Q)\leq C_{d}\,\frac{\mu(2B_{Q})}{\ell(Q)^{n}},

for
C_{d}=4A_{0}^{n}
. We write

Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{P}}.

Notice that

{\mathcal{P}}(Q)\approx_{C_{0}}\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset Q}\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{R}).

and thus saying that
Q
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling implies that

\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset Q}\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{R})\leq C_{d}^{\prime}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{Q})

for some
C_{d}^{\prime}
depending on
C_{d}
. Conversely, the latter condition implies that
Q
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling with another constant
C_{d}
depending on
C_{d}^{\prime}
.

From the properties of the David-Mattila lattice, we deduce the following.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that
C_{0}
and
A_{0}
are chosen suitably. If
Q
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling, then
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}
.
Also, any cube
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
such that
R\cap 2Q\neq\varnothing
and
\ell(R)=A_{0}\ell(Q)
belongs to
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}
.

Proof.

Let
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{P}}
. Regarding the fist statement of the lemma, if
Q\not\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}
, by (
2.3
) we have

\mu(2B_{Q})\leq\mu(100B(Q))\leq C_{0}^{-l}\,\mu(100^{l+1}B(Q))\quad\mbox{for all $l\geq 1$ with $100^{l}\leq C_{0}$}.

In particular, if
Q^{\prime}
denotes the parent of
Q
,

\mu(2B_{Q})\leq C_{0}^{-l}\,\mu(2B_{Q^{\prime}})\quad\mbox{for all $l\geq 1$ with $100^{l}\leq C_{0}$}.

So,

(3.1)

\mu(2B_{Q})\leq C_{0}^{-c\log C_{0}}\,\mu(2B_{Q^{\prime}})

for some
c>0
. Using now that
Q
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling, we get

\mu(2B_{Q})\leq C_{0}^{-c\log C_{0}}\,C_{d}\,\frac{\ell(Q^{\prime})^{n+1}}{\ell(Q)^{n+1}}\mu(2B_{Q})=4C_{0}^{-c\log C_{0}}\,A_{0}^{2n+1}\mu(2B_{Q}).

Recall now that, as explained in Remark
2.2
, we assume that
A_{0}=C_{0}^{C(n)}
, for some constant

C(n)
depending just on
n
. Then, clearly, the preceding inequality fails if
C_{0}
is big enough, which gives the desired contradiction.

To prove the second statement of the lemma, suppose that
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}^{\mathcal{P}}
and let
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
be such that
R\cap 2Q\neq\varnothing
and
\ell(R)=A_{0}\ell(Q)
.
By the definition and the fact that
R\subset B_{R}
we get

|x_{Q}-x_{R}|\leq 3\ell(Q)+r(B_{R}).

Since

r(B_{R})=28\,r(R)\geq 28A_{0}^{-k+1}=\frac{1}{2}\,C_{0}^{-1}A_{0}\,\ell(Q)\geq 2500\,\ell(Q),

we deduce that

2B_{Q}\subset 2B_{R}.

If
R^{\prime}
denotes the parent of
R
and
Q^{\prime\prime\prime}
the great-grandparent of
Q
(so that
\ell(Q^{\prime\prime\prime})=A_{0}^{3}\ell(Q)=A_{0}\ell(R^{\prime})
), by an analogous argument we infer that

2B_{R^{\prime}}\subset 2B_{Q^{\prime\prime\prime}}.

Then, using also that
Q
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling, we obtain

\mu(2B_{R^{\prime}})\leq\mu(2B_{Q^{\prime\prime\prime}})\leq C_{d}\,\left(\frac{\ell(Q^{\prime\prime\prime})}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{n+1}\!\mu(2B_{Q})\leq C_{d}\,A_{0}^{3(n+1)}\,\mu(2B_{R})=4A_{0}^{4n+3}\,\mu(2B_{R}).

If
R\not\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}
, arguing as in (
3.1
), we infer that

\mu(2B_{R})\leq C_{0}^{-c\log C_{0}}\,\mu(2B_{R^{\prime}}),

which contradicts the previous statement if
C_{0}
is big enough (recalling that
A_{0}=C_{0}^{C(n)}
).
∎

Notice that, by the preceding lemma, if
Q
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling, then

\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset Q}\frac{\ell(Q)^{n+1}}{\ell(R)^{n+1}}\,\mu(2B_{R})\lesssim_{C_{d}}\mu(Q).

For technical reasons that will be more evident below, it is appropriate to consider a discrete version of the density
\theta_{\mu}
. Given
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
, we let

\Theta(Q)=A_{0}^{kn}\quad\mbox{ if\, $\dfrac{\mu(2B_{Q})}{\ell(Q)^{n}}\in[A_{0}^{kn},A_{0}^{(k+1)n})$}.

Clearly,
\Theta(Q)\approx\theta_{\mu}(2B_{Q})
.
Notice also that if
\Theta(Q)=A_{0}^{k}
and
P
is a son of
Q
, then

\frac{\mu(2B_{P})}{\ell(P)^{n}}\leq\frac{\mu(2B_{Q})}{\ell(P)^{n}}=A_{0}^{n}\,\frac{\mu(2B_{Q})}{\ell(Q)^{n}}.

Thus,

(3.2)

\Theta(P)\leq A_{0}^{n}\,\Theta(Q)\quad\mbox{ for every son $P$ of $Q$.}

Given
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
and
k\geq 1
, we denote by
{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)
the family of maximal cubes
P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
satisfying

(3.3)

\ell(P)<\ell(Q),\qquad\Theta(P)\geq A_{0}^{kn}\Theta(Q).

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
be
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling. Then, for
k\geq 4
, every
P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)
is also
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling
and moreover
\Theta(P)=A_{0}^{kn}\Theta(Q)
.

Remark that this lemma implies that, under the assumptions in the lemma,

(3.4)

\Theta(P)\approx A_{0}^{nk}\Theta(Q)\quad\mbox{ for all $P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)$
and all
$k\geq 1$.}

Proof.

First we show that
\Theta(P)=A_{0}^{kn}\Theta(Q)
.
The fact that
\Theta(P)\geq A_{0}^{kn}\,\Theta(Q)
is clear. To see the converse inequality,
denote by
{\widehat{Q}}
the parent of
Q
. Notice that any cube
S\subset 9Q
with
\ell(S)=\ell(Q)

satisfies

\frac{\mu(2B_{S})}{\ell(S)^{n}}\leq\frac{\mu\big(2B_{{\widehat{Q}}}\big)}{\ell(Q)^{n}}=A_{0}^{n}\frac{\mu(2B_{{\widehat{Q}}})}{\ell({\widehat{Q}})^{n}}\leq A_{0}^{n+1}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q)\leq C_{d}\,A_{0}^{n+1}\,\frac{\mu(2B_{Q})}{\ell(Q)^{n}}<A_{0}^{3n}\,\frac{\mu(2B_{Q})}{\ell(Q)^{n}}.

Therefore,

\Theta(S)\leq A_{0}^{3n}\,\Theta(Q).

As a consequence, if
P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)
with
k\geq 4
, then its parent
{\widehat{P}}
satisfies

\Theta({\widehat{P}})<A_{0}^{kn}\,\Theta(Q)
, which implies that
\Theta(P)\leq A_{0}^{kn}\,\Theta(Q)
.

To see that
P
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling,
we split

(3.5)

{\mathcal{P}}(P)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset P\\
\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)^{n+1}}\,\mu(2B_{R})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset P\\
\ell(R)>\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)^{n+1}}\,\mu(2B_{R}).

The cubes
R
in the first sum on the right hand side satisfy

\Theta(R)\leq\Theta(P)
, by the definition of
{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)
. Thus,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset P\\
\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)^{n+1}}\,\mu(2B_{R})\leq A_{0}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset P\\
\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)}\,\Theta(R)\leq 2A_{0}^{n}\,\Theta(P)\leq 2A_{0}^{n}\,\frac{\mu(2B_{P})}{\ell(P)^{n}}.

Concerning the last sum in (
3.5
), notice that the cubes
R
in that sum satisfy
\ell(R)>\ell(Q)
.
Using that
A_{0}\gg 1
, it follows easily that
2B_{R}\subset 2B_{R^{\prime}}
, where
R^{\prime}
is the cube containing
Q
such that
\ell(R^{\prime})=A_{0}\,\ell(R)
.
Consequently, denoting by
{\widehat{Q}}
the parent of
Q
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset P\\
\ell(R)>\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)^{n+1}}\,\mu(2B_{R})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{R^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R^{\prime}\supset{\widehat{Q}}}\frac{\ell(P)}{A_{0}^{-1}\ell(R^{\prime})}\,\frac{\mu(2B_{R^{\prime}})}{(A_{0}^{-1}\ell(R^{\prime}))^{n}}

\displaystyle=A_{0}^{n+1}\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\sum_{R^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R^{\prime}\supset{\widehat{Q}}}\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R^{\prime})^{n+1}}\,\mu(2B_{R^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}^{n}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q)\leq A_{0}^{n}C_{d}\,\frac{\mu(2Q)}{\ell(Q)^{n}}\leq A_{0}^{2n}C_{d}\,\Theta(Q)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{A_{0}^{2n}C_{d}}{A_{0}^{4n}}\,\Theta(P)\leq\frac{C_{d}}{A_{0}^{2n}}\,\frac{\mu(2B_{P})}{\ell(P)^{n}},

where in the last two lines we took into account that
\ell(P)\leq A_{0}^{-1}\ell(Q)
(because
P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)
for some
k\geq 4
), that

Q
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling, and again that
P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)
for some
k\geq 4
.

From the estimates above, we infer that

{\mathcal{P}}(P)\leq\bigg(2A_{0}^{n}+\frac{C_{d}}{A_{0}^{2n}}\bigg)\,\frac{\mu(2B_{P})}{\ell(P)^{n}}\leq C_{d}\,\frac{\mu(2B_{P})}{\ell(P)^{n}},

since
C_{d}=4A_{0}^{n}
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
Q_{0},Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{m}
be a family of cubes from
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
such that
Q_{j}
is a child of
Q_{j-1}
for
1\leq j\leq m
. Suppose that
Q_{j}
is not
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling for
1\leq j\leq m
.
Then

(3.6)

\frac{\mu(2B_{Q_{m}})}{\ell(Q_{m})^{n}}\leq A_{0}^{-m/2}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0}).

and

(3.7)

{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{m})\leq 2A_{0}^{-m/2}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0}).

Proof.

Let us denote

{\widetilde{\Theta}}(R)=\frac{\mu(2B_{R})}{\ell(R)^{n}},
so that

{\mathcal{P}}(Q)=\sum_{R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}:R\supset Q}\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\,{\widetilde{\Theta}}(R).

For
1\leq j\leq m
,
the fact that
Q_{j}
is not
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling implies that

(3.8)

{\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j})\leq\frac{1}{C_{d}}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{j})=\frac{1}{C_{d}}\Biggl(\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\frac{\ell(Q_{j})}{\ell(Q_{j-k})}\,{\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j-k})+\frac{\ell(Q_{j})}{\ell(Q_{0})}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})\Biggr).

We will prove (
3.6
) by induction on
j
. For
j=0
this is in an immediate consequence of the
definition of
{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})
. Suppose that (
3.6
) holds for
0\leq h\leq j
, with
j\leq m-1
, and let us
consider the case
j+1
. From (
3.8
) and the induction hypothesis we get

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C_{d}}\Biggl({\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1})+\sum_{k=1}^{j}\frac{\ell(Q_{j+1})}{\ell(Q_{j+1-k})}\,{\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1-k})+\frac{\ell(Q_{j+1})}{\ell(Q_{0})}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C_{d}}\Biggl({\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1})+\sum_{k=1}^{j}A_{0}^{-k}\,{\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1-k})+A_{0}^{-j-1}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})\Biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C_{d}}\Biggl({\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1})+\sum_{k=1}^{j}A_{0}^{-k}\,A_{0}^{(-j-1+k)/2}{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})+A_{0}^{-j-1}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})\Biggr)

Since

\sum_{k=1}^{j}A_{0}^{-k}\,A_{0}^{(-j-1+k)/2}=A_{0}^{(-j-1)/2}\sum_{k=1}^{j}A_{0}^{-k/2}\leq A_{0}^{-j/2},

we obtain

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C_{d}}\bigl({\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1})+A_{0}^{-j/2}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})+A_{0}^{-j-1}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C_{d}}\bigl({\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1})+2\,A_{0}^{-j/2}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})\bigr)

It is straightforward to check that this yields
{\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{j+1})\leq A_{0}^{-(j+1)/2}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})
.

The estimate (
3.7
) follows easily from (
3.6
):

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{m})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\frac{\ell(Q_{m})}{\ell(Q_{m-k})}\,{\widetilde{\Theta}}(Q_{m-k})+\frac{\ell(Q_{m})}{\ell(Q_{0})}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}A_{0}^{-k}\,A_{0}^{-(m-k)/2}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})+A_{0}^{-m}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})

\displaystyle\leq A^{-m/2}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}A_{0}^{-k/2}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})+A_{0}^{-m}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0})\leq 2\,A_{0}^{-m/2}\,{\mathcal{P}}(Q_{0}).

∎

3.2.
The energies
{\mathcal{E}}
,
{\mathcal{E}}^{H}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}
, and the cubes dominated from below

For given
\lambda\geq 1
and
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
, we consider the energy

{\mathcal{E}}(\lambda Q)=\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(\lambda Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{3/4}\Theta(P)^{2}\,\mu(P).

We also denote

{\mathcal{E}}^{H}(\lambda Q)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(\lambda Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{3/4}\Theta(P)^{2}\,\mu(P)

and

{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(\lambda Q)=\sup_{k\geq 1}\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(\lambda Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\!1/2}\Theta(P)^{2}\,\mu(P).

Lemma 3.4
.

For every
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}

we have

{\mathcal{E}}(9Q)\lesssim{\mathcal{E}}^{H}(9Q)\lesssim{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q).

Proof.

For a given
R\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)
, we denote by
{\rm Tree}_{H}(R)
the family of cubes from
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
that
are contained in
R
and are not contained in any cube from
{\mathsf{hd}}^{k+1}(Q)
.
Using that
\Theta(P)\lesssim\Theta(R)
for all
P\in{\rm Tree}_{H}(R)
(remember that we do not keep track of the implicit constants depending on
A_{0}
), we get

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(9Q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{R\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)}\,\sum_{P\in{\rm Tree}_{H}(R)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{3/4}\Theta(P)^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{R\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)}\Theta(R)^{2}\sum_{P\in{\rm Tree}_{H}(R)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{3/4}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{R\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(R)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{3/4}\Theta(R)^{2}\,\mu(R)={\mathcal{E}}^{H}(9Q).

To show
{\mathcal{E}}^{H}(9Q)\lesssim{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)
,
denote

m_{k}(Q)=\frac{1}{\ell(Q)}\max\{\ell(P):P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)\}.

Then we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{H}(9Q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{3/4}\Theta(P)^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}m_{k}(Q)^{1/4}\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\!1/2}\Theta(P)^{2}\,\mu(P)

To estimate
m_{k}(Q)
, observe that if
P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)
and
k\geq 4
, then

A_{0}^{kn}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{Q})\approx\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})\lesssim\frac{\ell(Q)^{n}}{\ell(P)^{n}}\,\theta_{\mu}(2B_{Q}).

Hence,

\ell(P)\lesssim A_{0}^{-k}\,\ell(Q)
, and thus, since this also holds in the case
1\leq k\leq 3
,

(3.9)

m_{k}(Q)\lesssim A_{0}^{-k}\qquad\mbox{ for all $k\geq 1$.}

Consequently,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{H}(9Q)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\geq 0}A_{0}^{-k/4}\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\!1/2}\theta_{\mu}(2B_{P})^{2}\,\mu(P)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\geq 0}A_{0}^{-k/4}{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)\approx{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q).

∎

Remark 3.5
.

For the record, notice that, given
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{P}}
and

(3.10)

m_{k}(Q)=\frac{1}{\ell(Q)}\max\{\ell(P):P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)\},

as shown in (
3.9
), it turns out that

(3.11)

m_{k}(Q)\leq C_{1}A_{0}^{-k}.

Given
M\gg 1
(we will choose
M>A_{0}^{2n}\gg 1
), we say that
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
is
M
-dominated from below
if
there exists some
k\geq 1
such that

(3.12)

\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{hd}}^{k}(Q)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(9Q)}\left(\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right)^{\!1/2}\Theta(P)^{2}\,\mu(P)>M^{2}\,\Theta(Q)^{2}\,\mu(9Q),

or in other words,

(3.13)

{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)>M^{2}\,\Theta(Q)^{2}\,\mu(9Q),

We denote by
{\mathsf{DB}}(M)
the family of cubes from
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{P}}
that are
M
-dominated from below.
Notice that the cubes from
{\mathsf{DB}}(M)
are assumed to be
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling.

3.3.
The Main Propositions and the proof of Theorem
1.1

The proof of Theorem
1.1
consists of two main propositions. The first one is the following.

Main Proposition 3.6

(First Main Proposition)
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}

with compact support which has polynomial growth of degree
n
with constant
\theta_{0}
, that is,

\mu(B(x,r))\leq\theta_{0}\,r^{n}\quad\mbox{ for all $x\in\operatorname{supp}\mu$ and all $r>0$}.

Suppose also that
\|{\mathcal{R}}_{*}\mu\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}<\infty
. Then, for any choice of
M>1
,

(3.14)

\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}}\beta_{\mu,2}(2B_{Q})^{2}\,\Theta(Q)\,\mu(Q)\leq C\,\big(\|{\mathcal{R}}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\theta_{0}^{2}\,\|\mu\|+\sum_{Q\in{\mathsf{DB}}(M)}{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)\big),

with
C
depending on
M
.

The second main ingredient of the proof of Theorem
1.1
is the following.

Main Proposition 3.7

(Second Main Proposition)
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}

with compact support which has polynomial growth of degree
n
with constant
\theta_{0}
and such that
that
\|{\mathcal{R}}_{*}\mu\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}<\infty
. Let
M_{0}=A_{0}^{k_{0}n}
, where
k_{0}
is some big enough absolute constant depending just on
n
.
Then

\sum_{Q\in{\mathsf{DB}}(M_{0})}{\mathcal{E}}_{\infty}(9Q)\leq C\,\big(\|{\mathcal{R}}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\theta_{0}^{2}\,\|\mu\|\big),

where
C
depends just on
n
and the parameters of the dyadic lattice
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
.

By combining the two Main Propositions and choosing
M
and
M_{0}
appropriately, we deduce that

\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}}\beta_{2,\mu}(2B_{Q})^{2}\,\Theta(Q)\,\mu(Q)\leq C\,\big(\|{\mathcal{R}}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\theta_{0}^{2}\,\|\mu\|\big),

which implies Theorem
1.1
in the case when
\mu
is compactly supported. The general case for
\mu
not compactly supported follows from a standard reduction argument, by considering
\mu|_{B(0,r)}
, where
B(0,r)
has small boundary (see Lemmas 9.43 and 9.44 from
[
To5
]
, for example), and letting
r\to\infty
.

The rest of the paper is devoted to the proof of the two Main Propositions.

Part 2: Tractable trees

4.
The cubes with moderate decrement of Wolff energy and the associated tractable trees

4.1.
The family
{\mathsf{MDW}}
and the enlarged cubes

We let

\Lambda=A_{0}^{k_{\Lambda}n},

where
k_{\Lambda}>4
is some positive large number that will be fixed below, depending just on
n
and the
{\mathsf{DB}}
-parameter
M
. Its dependence on
M
is of the form

(4.1)

\Lambda\geq\max(CM,M^{\frac{8n-1}{8n-2}}),

for some big dimensional constant
C
.

Given
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{P}}
, we denote

{\mathsf{HD}}(R)={\mathsf{hd}}^{k_{\Lambda}}(R).

Also, we take
\delta_{0}\in(0,\Lambda^{-4n^{2}})
. In fact, the precise value of
\delta_{0}
will be fixed at the beginning of Section

7
.
We let
{\mathsf{LD}}(R)
be the family of cubes
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}

which
are maximal and satisfy

\ell(Q)<\ell(R)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad{\mathcal{P}}(Q)\leq\delta_{0}\,\Theta(R).

Next we introduce an “optional” family. For the proof of the First Main Proposition, we will take
{\mathsf{OP}}(R)=\varnothing
, while for the proof
of the Second Main Proposition we will take
{\mathsf{OP}}(R)={\mathsf{NDB}}(R)
, where

{\mathsf{NDB}}(R)
(which stands for “near
{\mathsf{DB}}
”) is the family of cubes
Q
which do not belong to
{\mathsf{LD}}(R)\cup{\mathsf{HD}}(R)
and satisfy the following:

•

\ell(Q)<\lambda\,\ell(R)
, with
\lambda=c_{3}\,\Lambda^{-4}
as in (
11.12
), and

•

there exists another cube
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{DB}}(M)
of the same generation as
Q
such that
Q^{\prime}\subset 20Q
.

We let
{\mathsf{Bad}}(R)
be the family of maximal cubes from
{\mathsf{LD}}(R)\cup{\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cup{\mathsf{OP}}(R)
(not necessarily contained in
R
) and we denote

{\mathsf{Stop}}(R)={\mathsf{Bad}}(R)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(R).

Remark that the arguments in Part 2 (Sections
4
-
10
) are valid with both choices of the optional family
{\mathsf{OP}}(R)
. In Parts 2 and 3 we consider the
{\mathsf{DB}}
-parameter
M
to be fixed, and we will usually write
{\mathsf{DB}}
instead of
{\mathsf{DB}}(M)
.

For a family
I\subset{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
, we denote

\sigma(I)=\sum_{P\in I}\Theta(P)^{2}\,\mu(P).

We say that a cube
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
has moderate decrement of Wolff energy, and we write

R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
, if
R
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling and

(4.2)

\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{Stop}}(R))\geq B^{-1}\,\sigma(R),

where

B=\Lambda^{\frac{1}{100n}}.

For
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
, the fact that the cubes from family
{\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{Stop}}(R)
may be located close to
\operatorname{supp}\mu\setminus R

may cause problems when trying to obtain estimates involving the Riesz transform. For this reason we need to
introduce some “enlarged cubes”.
Given
j\geq 0
and
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k}
,
we let

e_{j}(R)=R\cup\bigcup Q,

where the last union runs over the cubes
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k+1}
such that

(4.3)

{\rm dist}(x_{R},Q)<\frac{\ell(R)}{2}+2j\ell(Q).

We say that
e_{j}(R)
is an enlarged cube.
Notice that, since
{\rm diam}(Q)\leq\ell(Q)
,

(4.4)

\operatorname{supp}\mu\cap B\big(x_{R},\tfrac{1}{2}\ell(R)+2j\ell(Q)\big)\subset e_{j}(R)\subset B\big(x_{R},\tfrac{1}{2}\ell(R)+(2j+1)\ell(Q)\big).

Also, we have

(4.5)

e_{j}(R)\subset 2R\quad\mbox{ for $0\leq j\leq\frac{3}{4}A_{0}$,}

since, for any
Q\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu,k+1}
satisfying (
4.3
), its parent satisfies
{\widehat{Q}}

{\rm dist}(x_{R},{\widehat{Q}})<\frac{\ell(R)}{2}+2jA_{0}^{-1}\ell({\widehat{Q}})\leq 2\ell(R).

For
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
, we let

{\mathsf{Stop}}(e_{j}(R))={\mathsf{Bad}}(R)\cap{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(e_{j}(R)),

where
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(e_{j}(R))
stands for the family of cubes from
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
which are contained in

e_{j}(R)
and have side length at most
\ell(R)
.

Lemma 4.1
.

For any
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
there exists some
j
, with
10\leq j\leq A_{0}/4
such that

(4.6)

\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{Stop}}(e_{j}(R)))\leq B^{1/4}\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{Stop}}(e_{j-10}(R))),

assuming
A_{0}
big enough, depending just on
n
.

Proof.

Given
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
, suppose that such
j
does not exist. Let
j_{0}
be the largest integer which is multiple of
10
and smaller that
A_{0}/4
.
Then we get

\displaystyle\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{Stop}}(e_{j_{0}}(R)))

\displaystyle\geq B^{\frac{1}{4}}\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{Stop}}(e_{j_{0}-10}(R)))

\displaystyle\geq\ldots\geq\big(B^{\frac{1}{4}}\big)^{\frac{j_{0}}{10}-1}\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{Stop}}(R))\overset{\eqref{eq:MDWdef}}{\geq}B^{\frac{j_{0}}{40}-\frac{5}{4}}\sigma(R).

By (
4.5
), we have
e_{j_{0}}(R)\subset 2R
and thus

\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{Stop}}(e_{j_{0}}(R)))=\sum_{Q\in{\mathsf{HD}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{Stop}}(e_{j_{0}}(R))}\Lambda^{2}\Theta(R)^{2}\mu(Q)\leq\Lambda^{2}\Theta(R)^{2}\mu(2R).

Since
R
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling (and in particular
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{db}
), denoting by
{\widehat{R}}
the parent of
R
, we derive

(4.7)

\mu(2R)\leq\mu(2B_{{\widehat{R}}})\leq\frac{\ell({\widehat{R}})^{n+1}}{\ell(R)}\,{\mathcal{P}}(R)\leq C_{d}\,A_{0}^{n+1}\mu(2B_{R})\leq C_{0}\,C_{d}\,A_{0}^{n+1}\mu(R).

So we deduce that

B^{\frac{j_{0}}{40}-\frac{5}{4}}\sigma(R)\leq C_{0}\,C_{d}\,A_{0}^{n+1}\,\Lambda^{2}\sigma(R),

or equivalently, recalling the choice of
B
and
C_{d}
,

\Lambda^{\frac{1}{100n}\left(\frac{j_{0}}{40}-\frac{5}{4}\right)-2}\leq 4C_{0}\,A_{0}^{2n+1}.

Since
\Lambda\geq A_{0}^{n}
and
j_{0}\approx A_{0}
, it is clear that this inequality is violated if
A_{0}
is big enough, depending just on
n
.
∎

Given
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
, let
j\geq 10
be minimal such that (
4.6
) holds. We denote

h(R)=j-10
and we write

e(R)=e_{h(R)}(R),\qquad e^{\prime}(R)=e_{h(R)+1}(R),\quad e^{\prime\prime}(R)=e_{h(R)+2}(R),\quad e^{(k)}(R)=e_{h(R)+k}(R),

for
k\geq 1
.
We let

\displaystyle B(e(R))

\displaystyle=B\big(x_{R},(\tfrac{1}{2}+2A_{0}^{-1}h(R))\ell(R)\big),

\displaystyle B(e^{\prime}(R))

\displaystyle=B\big(x_{R},(\tfrac{1}{2}+2A_{0}^{-1}(h(R)+1))\ell(R)\big),

\displaystyle B(e^{\prime\prime}(R))

\displaystyle=B\big(x_{R},(\tfrac{1}{2}+2A_{0}^{-1}(h(R)+2))\ell(R)\big),

\displaystyle B(e^{(k)}(R))

\displaystyle=B\big(x_{R},(\tfrac{1}{2}+2A_{0}^{-1}(h(R)+k))\ell(R)\big).

By construction (see (
4.4
)) we have

B(e^{\prime}(R))\cap\operatorname{supp}\mu\subset e^{\prime}(R),

and analogously replacing
e^{\prime}(R)
by
e(R)
or
e^{\prime\prime}(R)
.
Remark also that

e(R)\subset B(e^{\prime}(R))\quad\text{ and }\quad{\rm dist}(e(R),\partial B(e^{\prime}(R)))\geq A_{0}^{-1}\ell(R),

and, analogously,

e^{\prime}(R)\subset B(e^{\prime\prime}(R))\quad\text{ and }\quad{\rm dist}(e^{\prime}(R),\partial B(e^{\prime\prime}(R)))\geq A_{0}^{-1}\ell(R).

Lemma 4.2
.

For each
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
we have

B(e^{\prime\prime}(R))\subset(1+8A_{0}^{-1})\,\,B(e(R))\subset B(e^{(6)}(R)),

and more generally, for
k\geq 2
such that
h(R)+k-2\leq A_{0}/2
,

B(e^{(k)}(R))\subset(1+8A_{0}^{-1})\,\,B(e^{(k-2)}(R))\subset B(e^{(k+4)}(R)).

Also,

B(e^{(10)}(R))\subset B\big(x_{R},\tfrac{3}{2}\ell(R)\big).

Proof.

This follows from straightforward calculations.
Indeed,

r(B(e^{(k)}(R)))=\frac{(\tfrac{1}{2}+2A_{0}^{-1}(h(R)+k))\ell(R)}{(\tfrac{1}{2}+2A_{0}^{-1}(h(R)+k-2))\ell(R)}\,r(B(e^{(k-2)}(R)))\\
=1+\frac{8A_{0}^{-1}}{1+4A_{0}^{-1}(h(R)+k-2)}\,r(B(e^{(k-2)}(R)))\leq(1+8A_{0}^{-1})\,r(B(e^{(k-2)}(R))).

Also, using that
h(R)+k-2\leq A_{0}/2
,

\displaystyle(1+8A_{0}^{-1})\,r(B(e^{(k-2)}(R)))

\displaystyle=(1+8A_{0}^{-1})\,\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+2A_{0}^{-1}(h(R)+k-2)\big)\,\ell(R)

\displaystyle\leq\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+2A_{0}^{-1}(h(R)+k-2)+4A_{0}^{-1}+8A_{0}^{-1}\big)\,\ell(R)

\displaystyle=r(B(e^{(k+4)}(R))).

The last statement of the lemma follows from the fact that
h(R)+10\leq A_{0}/4<A_{0}/2
:

B(e^{(10)}(R))=B\big(x_{R},(\tfrac{1}{2}+2A_{0}^{-1}(h(R)+10))\ell(R)\big)\subset B\big(x_{R},(\tfrac{1}{2}+2)\ell(R)\big)=B\big(x_{R},\tfrac{3}{2}\ell(R)\big).

∎

4.2.
Generalized trees and negligible cubes

Next we need to define some families that can be considered as “generalized trees”. First, we introduce some additional notation regarding the stopping cubes. For
R\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{P}}
we set

{\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(R)={\mathsf{Stop}}(R)\cap{\mathsf{HD}}(R).

Assume additionally that
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
. We write
{\mathsf{Stop}}(e(R))={\mathsf{Stop}}(e_{h(R)}(R))
and
{\mathsf{Stop}}(e^{\prime}(R))={\mathsf{Stop}}(e_{h(R)+1}(R))
. Furthermore,

{\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(e(R))={\mathsf{Stop}}(e(R))\cap{\mathsf{HD}}(R),

and

{\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(e^{\prime}(R))={\mathsf{Stop}}(e^{\prime}(R))\cap{\mathsf{HD}}(R).

We define
{\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(e^{(k)}(R))
for
2\leq k\leq 10
analogously. Also, we set

{\mathsf{HD}}_{2}(e^{\prime}(R))=\bigcup_{Q\in{\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(e^{\prime}(R))}({\mathsf{Stop}}(Q)\cap{\mathsf{HD}}(Q))

and

(4.8)

{\mathsf{Stop}}_{2}(e^{\prime}(R))=\big({\mathsf{Stop}}(e^{\prime}(R))\setminus{\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(e^{\prime}(R))\big)\cup\bigcup_{Q\in{\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(e^{\prime}(R))}{\mathsf{Stop}}(Q).

We let
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{Stop}}(e^{\prime}(R))
be the family of cubes made up of
R
and all the cubes of the next generations which are contained in
e^{\prime}(R)
but are not
strictly contained in any cube from
{\mathsf{Stop}}_{2}(e^{\prime}(R))
.

Observe that the defining property of
{\mathsf{MDW}}
(
4.2
) can now be rewritten as

(4.9)

\sigma(R)\leq B\,\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(R)).

Moreover, by (
4.6
) and the definition of
e(R)
we have

(4.10)

\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(e^{(10)}(R)))\leq B^{1/4}\sigma({\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(e(R))).

We define now the family of negligible cubes. We say that a cube
Q\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{Stop}}(e^{\prime}(R))
is negligible for
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{Stop}}(e^{\prime}(R))
, and we write
Q\in{\mathsf{Neg}}(e^{\prime}(R))
if
there does not exist any cube from
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathsf{Stop}}(e^{\prime}(R))
that contains
Q
and is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
R\in{\mathsf{MDW}}
. If
Q\in{\mathsf{Neg}}(e^{\prime}(R))
, then
Q\subset e^{\prime}(R)\setminus R
,
Q
is not contained in any cube from
{\mathsf{HD}}_{1}(e^{\prime}(R))
, and

(4.11)

\ell(Q)\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{2}\,\ell(R).

Proof.

Let
Q\in{\mathsf{Neg}}(e^{\prime}(R))
. We have
Q\subset e^{\prime}(R)\setminus R
due to the fact that
R
is
{\mathcal{P}}
-doubling.
